...another, more proximate <term>mercury<note>In alchemical theory, mercury is the "spirit" or transformative principle of a metal. "Proximate" mercury is the essence already partially refined within the gold or silver itself.</note></term>, which is enclosed within the body of gold and silver; this must be drawn out by the first and "remote" mercury. Thus, through this raw power, you will draw out a power that is warmer, moist, airy, subtle, and congealed into a metallic form.

"Our <term>quicksilver<note>Original French: 'argent-vif'. Alchemists often used this term to distinguish their refined mercury from the common liquid metal found in nature.</note></term>," he says elsewhere, "is the very body of gold, liquefied and purified"; he repeats this in a hundred places, as do his two companions and many others.

The quicksilver of gold, which is properly its entire substance, is that quicksilver which <term>Hermes<note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the father of alchemy and the author of the Emerald Tablet.</note></term> says is hidden within the golden caverns. more obscurely, he speaks of the two sometimes as distinct, and sometimes as united, and at another time as already fixed and coagulated into stone; and this is what the Reader must examine with attention.

Common <term>mercury<note>Also known as quicksilver, the common liquid metal.</note></term> does not dissolve gold and silver in such a way that it [the mercury] might be separated from them very easily; but the mercury of the <term>Philosophers<note>The "Philosophers" are the alchemical masters; their mercury is a refined, spiritualized substance rather than the raw metal found in mines.</note></term> dissolves gold and silver, and can no longer be separated from them, any more than water mixed with other water. Common mercury has within itself a combustible and "bad" <term>sulfur<note>In alchemical theory, sulfur represents the "soul" or active fire of a substance. "Combustible" sulfur was viewed as an impurity that caused metals to tarnish or decay.</note></term>, by which metals are rendered vile and dirty; but our <term>quicksilver<note>Original: 'argent-vif'. A literal translation of the Latin 'argentum vivum'.</note></term> has within itself an incombustible, fixed, and perfect sulfur, very white and very red. ...they also call it our <term>copper<note>In alchemy, copper is often associated with Venus and represents an intermediate state of the metal's transformation.</note></term>, or our <term>incombustible sulfur<note>A term for the "fixed" or stabilized essence of the substance that remains stable even when subjected to intense fire.</note></term>: just as when it has reached the white stage, they say it is their <term>arsenic<note>In a philosophical context, "arsenic" refers not to the common poison, but to a purifying agent or the "spirit" that whitens the matter.</note></term> or their <term>philosophical Moon<note>A name for the substance when it has achieved a silver-like purity and whiteness, signaling the penultimate stage of the Great Work.</note></term>; and because this Moon is transparent and white like salt, they call it salt; and according to the colors, properties, or <term>accidents<note>In early modern philosophy, "accidents" are the outward characteristics (like color or smell) that are not part of the substance's essential nature.</note></term>, they give it the names of every kind of salt—of <term>vitriol<note>A general term for sulfate minerals, often used to describe the corrosive or acidic nature of the early matter.</note></term>, of <term>alum<note>A mineral used here to describe the stabilizing or "binding" qualities of the Stone.</note></term>, of <term>sal ammoniac<note>A volatile salt used by alchemists to represent the ability of the spirit to rise and sublimate.</note></term>, and other such things: and finally, because according to various perceptions it can be compared to all things in the world, they also give it the name of all things in the world, depending on the different applications that can be made of the substance in the various states in which it is found.

The Author applies this; for it is quite true that they do not write to teach the art, but as they say themselves, it is only to bear witness that the <term>science<note>In this context, "science" refers to the "Sacred Art" or alchemy, which practitioners viewed as the ultimate understanding of nature's hidden laws.</note></term> is true. Very often they speak without disguise, yet they shroud the truth in so many enigmas and ambiguities that one must possess great insight to understand them; as <term>mercury<note>The "mercury of the philosophers" is the central solvent and spirit of the Great Work. It is distinct from common, metallic quicksilver and is considered the most guarded secret of the art.</note></term> is the key to the entire <term>work<note>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) refers to the entire laboratory process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term>, it is also the subject they strive to make the most obscure and difficult.

   Although I do not believe it is strictly necessary to prove what I have said regarding the darkness in which the Reader walks during these readings, and although I could rely on what several Authors have said to prove that the clarification I have provided is well-founded and extracted...

> The Philosophers have hidden the materials of their Secret from the greedy under the ambiguous names of <term>sulfur<note>One of the "Three Primes" (Tria Prima) of alchemy, representing the active, masculine, and combustible principle of matter.</note></term> and <term>mercury<note>The feminine, passive, and volatile principle. Alchemists distinguished between "common" mercury (quicksilver) and their "philosophical" mercury, which was the secret solvent of the Work.</note></term>. The most discerning readers, through the study of the best Authors, have understood that under the name of sulfur they mean the <term>Sun<note>In this context, the "Sun" (Sol) refers to the solar principle or the essence of gold, believed to contain a "fixed" or incombustible fire.</note></term>, from which the true incombustible sulfur is extracted; but they are still in doubt regarding mercury; for it is shrouded in so many obscurities and so many ambiguous names, and with so many twists and turns, that it is very difficult to understand what they mean, and what the true matter of this mercury is: and in order to make it even more obscure, they present us with several and

<page-num>272</page-num>
<header>->Secrets of the Philosophy of the Ancients,<-</header>

The Author makes the application; for it is quite true that they do not write to teach the <term>Art<note>Refers to Alchemy, often called the "Hermetic Art" or the "Royal Art" by its practitioners.</note></term>, but as they themselves say, it is only to bear witness that the science is true: very often they speak without disguise, but they shroud the truth with so many enigmas and ambiguities, that one must have great penetration to understand them; as <term>mercury<note>Specifically "Philosophical Mercury," the essential starting matter or solvent of the Great Work, distinct from common quicksilver.</note></term> is the key to the whole <term>work<note>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus), the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term>; it is also that which they strive to make most obscure and most difficult.

## VIIth TREATISE. <note>The OCR reads "IIV^e," which is likely a printer's error for VII^e (7th).</note>

### *Of several other ambiguities & parables of fire.*

**A**s this science cannot be taught clearly, the **Philosophers** <note>In alchemical texts, "Philosophers" refers to the ancient masters of the "Hermetic Art" who were believed to possess the secret of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> were obliged to make use of parables and riddles to leave it to be guessed by those who are curious about it; and because all men are of different temperaments, there have been authors who have treated it diversely, according to the greater or lesser subtlety of mind that they possessed, which led them to consider in this **Work** <note>The "Work" (or *Magnum Opus*) refers to the complex laboratory process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> certain actions of nature that others did not notice. Most of the Philosophers took pride in these subtleties, for the vanity that is natural to all men leads us to strive to make

<sig>iii d</sig>

<vocab>alchemy, philosophy, parables, fire, nature, subtlety, mind</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that alchemical knowledge is intentionally hidden behind riddles and parables because it cannot be taught openly, noting that different masters emphasized different aspects of the "Work" based on their own insights.</summary>
<keywords>alchemical parables, secrecy, nature, subtlety of mind, the Great Work, vanity of authors</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 294 regarding the motives behind alchemical secrecy. The previous page ended by explaining that authors used parables to hide their secrets from the unworthy, while also satisfying their own vanity.</meta>

<page-num>295</page-num>
<header>uncovered. <note>original: "découverts." This word likely completes a sentence from the preceding page.</note></header>

...to appear that we have more wit and more insight than others, and even a more particular skill in knowing how to tell the truth under terms that the common man does not understand. Those who know the art understand these subtili-
<margin>3</margin>
-ties well, because they possess the key to the riddle; but those who study to attain this **divine science** <note>A common term for Alchemy, reflecting the belief that the "Great Work" was a sacred process requiring divine favor.</note> find themselves quite confused by them: now, as the **Chemical Philosophers** <note>The "Philosophes Chymistes" were the masters of alchemy who sought the Philosopher's Stone.</note> seem to have worked in competition with one another to invent these subtle parables and riddles, I shall explain some of them that seem to me the most important, among which is that of **fire**.

The **Chemical Philosophers** have several kinds of fires: there are some they name and which are in fact intrinsic fires, and oth-

The internal fires of this art are also several: Raymond Lully <note>Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher and polymath. A large body of alchemical literature was attributed to him posthumously, forming the "Pseudo-Lullian" corpus which was highly influential in the Renaissance.</note> speaks of three; the natural fire, the unnatural fire, and another which he calls the fire against nature.

<margin>Series of the Testament, chapter 6. <note>original: "Serie Te-stam. cap. 6." This refers to the *Testamentum*, a foundational text of the Pseudo-Lullian alchemical tradition.</note></margin>
The **natural fire** is that which is regarded as the natural and internal heat of the matter of the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher’s Stone, the elusive substance alchemists sought to create to transmute metals and achieve longevity.</note>, and particularly the internal heat of gold, which, once dissolved, is the fire of its vivifying **sulfur** <note>In alchemical theory, Sulfur is the active, masculine principle representing heat and dry "combustibility," while Mercury is the passive, feminine principle.</note>, which cooks, digests, and perfects the entire work; this is why some Philosophers have said that their work was cooked in the heat of the Sun; a statement which the ignorant have taken literally.

### Of Vessels.

<margin>3</margin>
The name "vessel" is also ambiguous, for there is the **vessel of art** and that of **nature**. The vessel of art is a simple glass vessel, such as most of the **Philosophers** <note>The historical authors of alchemical treatises.</note> describe it: round and oval.

But the **vessel of nature** is the most important and the most difficult to find; it is properly the feminine vessel that is called the *matrix*, in which the **King** <note>A symbolic name for the primary metallic or spiritual substance, often associated with gold or sulfur.</note> must corrupt himself <note>In alchemy, "corruption" or "putrefaction" is the necessary stage of decomposition that allows for a substance to be reborn in a perfected state.</note> and spread his seed, in order to produce the **philosophical child** <note>A common symbol for the Philosopher’s Stone, representing the successful union of opposites.</note> there.

<summary>This page concludes the instructions on physical heat sources and introduces the duality of alchemical vessels, distinguishing the physical glass container from the symbolic "vessel of nature" or matrix.</summary>
<keywords>external fire, charcoal fire, lamp fire, vessel of art, vessel of nature, matrix, the King, philosophical child, alchemy</keywords>

They also call this **natural vessel** the **vessel of art**, because it is the only means by which the art is accomplished.

And as this vessel is at the same time a liquid, they call this liquid *menstruum* <note>original: "menstrue"; in alchemical theory, this refers to a solvent or "solvent-matter" that dissolves and nourishes the metallic seed, named by analogy to the biological fluid believed to nourish a fetus.</note>, insofar as it produces the same effect as the feminine *menstruum*, which provides growth to the **masculine seed** which changes into its nature, just as that of the male does with respect to that of the female: this is why the **Philosophers** so often compare the union of **gold** and **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury; in this context, it represents the volatile, feminine principle that must be joined with the "fixed" masculine principle of gold.</term> to marriage and human generation.

They have even created an ambiguity regarding the **seal of the vessel**; for they say that the vessel must be sealed **hermetically**: now the **seal of Hermes** has a double meaning. The literal sense is to seal the glass vessel; in such a way

<page-num>304</page-num>
<header>Secrets of the Philosophy of the Ancients,</header>

[when] it is liquid and flowing, he <note>Referring to the "Philosopher" or alchemist.</note> calls it mercury: if it rises in vapors, he calls it smoke, spirit, wind, air, and even fire; and if it precipitates, he names it water, **May dew** <note>original: "rosée du mois de Mai"; alchemists believed May dew was charged with celestial energy or "universal spirit," making it a prized solvent.</note>, urine, etc. And if he sees that which is at the bottom rise to the surface of the matter, he calls it sublimations, distillations, filtrations: but when it is dry at the white stage, they call it talc, and by the name of all salts, or of arsenic or marble, and of everything that is white. And when it is red, they give it the name of sulfur, of orpiment <term>orpiment: a deep yellow-to-orange arsenic sulfide mineral, used as a pigment and in alchemical attempts to create gold.</term>, ruby, copper, cinnabar <term>cinnabar: a bright red mineral (mercury sulfide), the most common source of elemental mercury in nature.</term>, and of everything that might resemble it. The name *our* <note>original: "notre"</note> which they usually add to it, indicates that it is not the **vulgar** <note>In alchemy, "vulgar" refers to common, commercial-grade substances found in shops, as opposed to the purified or "philosophical" versions prepared by the alchemist.</note> substance; and thus *our sulfur*, *our cinnabar*, *our orpiment*, and the rest, shows the difference there is between the common substance and that of which they speak.

### *Of the names of the Stone.*

The **Stone** can be considered either as being in the state of being made—that is to say, still liquid—or else as being already perfect and dry. In these two states, it takes the name of all the things to which the **Philosopher** <note>The alchemist or practitioner of the Great Work.</note> who makes it can or wishes to compare it, at the time he observes it; so that while—

### On some other parables & enigmas.

As it would be very dangerous to teach this science <note>In this context, "science" refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy—the process of transmuting base metals into gold or achieving spiritual perfection.</note> plainly and straightforwardly, and because all the **Chemical Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophes Chymistes"; a term for alchemists who combined laboratory practice with philosophical and spiritual inquiry.</note> who have written about it have tried to make it known under certain figures or enigmatic parables, by which <margin>3</margin> the **wise** can nonetheless easily enough understand what they mean; I will report a few of them, so that one may see that when one applies oneself, possesses good sense, and is initiated into the natural foundations of **Philosophy**, these enigmas are not difficult to explain, assuming that the Reader has read the Books of the Philosophers, and thus already has some knowledge of these enigmas. I will not dwell at great length—

Alchemy was often described through the metaphor of cooking or digestion.</note>

In the first place, these two substances must be made fit for use by perfectly purifying the **bodies** <note>In alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to the solid metals, specifically gold or silver, which act as the "seed" of the work.</note> through **cupellation** <note>A refining process where ores are heated in a shallow cup (a cupel) to separate precious metals from base metals like lead.</note> or by **parting**, <note>original: "départ." A method of separating gold from silver, typically using an acid like aqua fortis.</note> so that no impure metal remains. Some have used them reduced to **calx**, <note>original: "chaux." A fine powder produced by heating a metal until it oxidizes or crumbles.</note> while others have taken them in leaves so that **dissolution** <note>The process of dissolving the solid metal into a liquid state.</note> might be easier and faster; others have put them into **shot** <note>original: "grenaille." Small grains or pellets created by pouring molten metal into water.</note>: it is therefore of no importance in what state they are, provided the **solvent** <note>The substance used to dissolve the metal.</note> is well prepared.

And in the second place, the **mercury** must be made so **subtle** <note>Highly refined, active, or volatile.</note> that it can **re-incruder** <note>original: "réincruder." A technical alchemical term for reversing the "growth" of a metal, returning "mature" gold to its "crude" or raw, liquid state.</note> the gold, and reduce it to its primary nature of quicksilver.

When this happens, these two **quicksilvers**—which are then only a single matter—form the **philosophical mercury**, which is designated by the mystical **caduceus** <note>The staff of Hermes (Mercury) entwined with two serpents. In this manner and by this regimen, one see clearly enough that the fixed becomes volatile at the beginning through union with the volatile, and that subsequently the volatile becomes fixed through union with the fixed; and that from these two substances of the **body** and the **spirit**, there results a third matter which partakes of the fixity of the body on one hand, and of the **subtlety** and **penetration** <note>The ability of the Stone to permeate and transform every part of a base metal.</note> of the spirit on the other, which flows like wax at the heat of fire, and which at the same time resists all—

purification, and even of making the **Stone** <term>Stone <gloss>The "Philosopher's Stone," the ultimate goal of alchemy, believed to be a substance that could transmute base metals into gold and offer longevity.</gloss></term>; but if that were so, it would always be necessary for the essential materials to be the same, that is to say, the **perfect body** <gloss>In alchemical literature, the "perfect body" refers to gold, the most stable of the metals.</gloss> and **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver <gloss>Commonly known as mercury; alchemists viewed it as the "mother" or "spirit" of all metals.</gloss></term>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What could persuade one of this would be, among others, the *Books of Experiments* of **Raymond Lully** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315). A Majorcan philosopher whose name was later attached to a vast collection of alchemical texts he did not actually write.</note>, the manual techniques, and the *Book of Tinctures* of **Basil Valentine** <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, often cited as a monk from Erfurt, though the name was likely a pseudonym for the publisher Johann Thölde.</note>, the works of **Ripley** <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), an English canon and one of the most famous alchemists of the 15th century.</note>, of **Parisinus** <note>Christopher Parisinus, an Italian author associated with the Lullian tradition of alchemy.</note>, and other authors of the School of Raymond Lully, and above all of **Paracelsus** <note>The revolutionary Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541) who transitioned alchemy toward medicine (iatrochemistry).</note>, and finally of **Philalethes** <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes (the "peaceful lover of truth"), the pseudonym of the 17th-century alchemist George Starkey.</note>. <margin>f 3</margin> It could be that the Moderns have perfected this art by finding easier and faster ways, since it is easy to perfect an art once it is known.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Be that as it may, I advise any man curious about this science not to involve himself in it,

->Ee<-

# APPROBATION

->From **MR. ANDRY**, Royal Counselor-Reader & Professor, Doctor-Regent of the Faculty of Medicine of Paris, & Royal Censor of Books.<-

By order of My Lord the <term>Keeper of the Seals <gloss>The "Garde des Sceaux," the head of the French judicial system, who oversaw the granting of printing rights.</gloss></term>, I have examined this Manuscript entitled *The Most Hidden Secrets of the Philosophy of the Ancients, Discovered & Explained, etc.* by the <term>Lord <gloss>The title "Sieur" was an honorific for a gentleman or a man of some social standing.</gloss></term> CROSSET DE LA HEAUMERIE. I have found nothing therein that might prevent its printing. Done at Paris, this 19th of November, 1719. ANDRY. <note>Nicolas Andry (1658–1742) was a famous physician, often called the father of orthopedics, who also served as a royal censor.</note>

---

# ROYAL PRIVILEGE

Louis, by the grace of God, King of France & Navarre; To our beloved and loyal Counselors, the members of our Courts of Parliament, <term>Masters of Requests <gloss>High-ranking judicial officers who handled petitions to the King’s Council.</gloss></term> in ordinary of our Household, Great Council, Provost of Paris, Bailiffs, Seneschals, their Civil Lieutenants, and our other Justices whom it may concern: Greetings. Our well-beloved CHARLES-MAURICE D’HOURY the younger, Bookseller in Paris, having informed Us that there has been placed in his hands a Manuscript entitled *The Most Hidden Secrets of the Philosophy of the Ancients, Discovered & Explained, following a most curious History*, by the Lord Crosset de la Heaumerie, which he wishes to have printed and given to the Public, if it

<summary>This page contains the official "Approbation" from the royal censor, Mr. Andry, and the beginning of the "Royal Privilege" (publishing license) granted by King Louis XV for the book's publication in 1719.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the text of the "Royal Privilege" initiated on page 334. It outlines the specific legal protections and commercial rights granted by King Louis XV to the publisher Charles-Maurice d’Houry for the printing of Crosset de la Heaumerie’s work on ancient philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>335</page-num>
<margin>f. 3</margin>

...[It] has pleased us to grant him our <term>Letters of Privilege <gloss>An official document granting an exclusive right to publish a specific work, similar to a modern copyright.</gloss></term> necessary for this purpose. For these reasons, wishing to treat the said <term>Petitioner <gloss>Referring to Charles-Maurice d’Houry, the bookseller who requested the license.</gloss></term> favorably, We have permitted and do permit him by these Presents to have the said Book printed in such volumes, format, margin, and typeface, together or separately, and as many times as he sees fit; and to sell, have sold, and distribute it throughout our entire Kingdom during the term of three consecutive years, counting from the day of the date of these Presents.

We forbid all types of persons, of whatever quality and condition they may be, to introduce any foreign printing of this work into any place under our rule: as well as to all Booksellers, Printers, and others, to print, have printed, sell, have sold, distribute, or counterfeit the said Book in whole or in part, or to make any extracts from it, under any pretext whatsoever—whether of addition, correction, change of title, or otherwise—without the express permission in writing from the said Petitioner, or those who hold rights from him.

This is under penalty of confiscation of the counterfeit copies, and a fine of <note>1,500 livres was a substantial sum in 1719, roughly equivalent to several years' wages for a skilled laborer.</note> fifteen hundred livres against each of the offenders, of which one third shall go to Us, one third to the **Hôtel-Dieu of Paris**, and the other third to the said Petitioner, along with all costs, damages, and interests.

This is on the condition that these Presents shall be recorded in full in the Register of the Guild of Booksellers and Printers of Paris, within three months of their date; that the printing of this Book shall be done in our Kingdom, and not elsewhere, on good paper and in fine typefaces, in accordance with the <term>Regulations of the Book Trade <gloss>The "Réglemens de la Librairie" were strict laws governing the quality and legality of all printed materials in France.</gloss></term>; and that before...

<summary>This page details the legal protections of the Royal Privilege, granting the publisher exclusive rights for three years and establishing heavy fines for counterfeiters, a portion of which was donated to the city's main hospital.</summary>

Concerning the contents of which we command and enjoin you to allow the Petitioner or those holding his rights to enjoy fully and peacefully, without suffering any trouble or hindrance to be caused to them. We desire that the copy of these Presents, which shall be printed in full at the beginning or at the end of the said Book, be held as duly notified, and that copies collated by one of our beloved and loyal Councilors and Secretaries be given the same trust as the Original. We command the first of our Ushers or Sergeants to carry out all required and necessary acts for the execution of these presents, without asking for further permission, and notwithstanding any <term>Clamor of Haro <gloss>An ancient legal custom, particularly in Normandy, where a person could cry "Haro!" to immediately halt an injustice or property dispute.</gloss></term>, <term>Norman Charter <gloss>A royal document from 1315 that protected the specific legal customs of Normandy against the overreach of the French crown.</gloss></term>, and Letters to the contrary; for such is our pleasure. Given at Paris the tenth day of the month of July, the year of grace one thousand seven hundred and twenty-two, and the seventh year of our Reign. By the King in his Council,

## FIRST TREATISE.

### *On metallic seeds.*

There is no one, however little **enlightened** <note>original: "éclairé." This refers to the intellectual light of the Enlightenment period, suggesting that even basic logic supports this view.</note> they may be, who does not know that the true **seed** <term>sémence: In alchemy, this is the generative principle or "blueprint" of a substance, rather than just a physical seed.</term> of a thing is neither the grain nor the sperm, but the **essential and constitutive matter** of such a being; that is to say, a certain mixture of the **subtle element** <note>original: "l’élément subtil." In alchemical theory, this is the pure, spiritualized substance—often associated with "mercury" or "spirit"—that gives life and form to physical matter.</note> in certain precise proportions, which make a thing what it is and grant it certain properties. This **seminal essence** is enveloped by other **gross elements** <note>original: "élémens grossiers." These are the physical, impure, or "dead" parts of a substance that hide its inner vital spirit.</note> that retain it, so that it does not evaporate due to its subtlety. One only has to see the manner in which **Chemists** <note>original: "Chymistes." During this era, the distinction between "chemist" and "alchemist" was still fluid; the author refers to those who perform laboratory extractions.</note> separate the essence of **vegetables** and **animals** from these gross elements, which, far from having any property of its essence, actually diminish its

What can further prove the strength of the **seminal essence** <note>seminal essence: A vital, generative power believed by early natural philosophers to reside in matter, directing its growth and specific form, much like biological DNA is understood today.</note>, which is sometimes found more powerful and vigorous in certain places and certain subjects than in others, is what we see in the same Treatise by Boyle <note>Referring to Robert Boyle’s *Suspicions about some Hidden Qualities of the Air* (1674).</note>. A curious friend of his <note>original: "amis curieux." In the 17th century, a *curieux* referred to a person of learning, a virtuoso, or a collector of scientific rarities.</note> showed him a piece of silver ore, around which he pointed out certain **vegetations** <note>vegetations: Branching, root-like structures of metal. To the authors of this period, the fact that metals could form these shapes suggested they grew in the earth like plants.</note> that had formed. He noted that the silver filaments he saw had been produced some time after this stone had come into his possession. This seems to stem from no other cause than that this stony earth was so filled with **metallic seed** <note>metallic seed: An alchemical concept (original: "sémence métallique") suggesting that metals contain a "seed" or "ferment" that can transform surrounding matter into more of the same metal.</note> that it had, like a magnet, attracted the air and changed it imperceptibly and little by little into its own nature as silver.

I will relate, in the form of a corollary <term>a logical consequence or additional illustration of a proven point</term>, a very well-known matter in Italy which—although it appears in some way different because it is not of a metallic nature—will nonetheless serve to make what I have just said even more apparent and better understood.

Four or five leagues <note>A league was a unit of distance; in 17th-century France, a "post league" was roughly 2.4 miles or 3.9 kilometers.</note> from Rome, not far from Tivoli, there is a mountain named *Poli*, belonging to the Duke of that name <note>The Conti family held the Duchy of Poli; their palace and the surrounding lands were famous for natural curiosities.</note>. In this mountain there are many stones which, although devoid of earth and apparently very dry, produce mushrooms of an extraordinary size when the dew and gentle rains of Spring moisten them. Some of these mushrooms weigh up to thirty pounds, and even more, and are very delicate <note>Meaning delicious or choice in flavor.</note> when they are gathered young. composed [of] two substances, one pure, the other impure. The pure substance is the seminal essence of the subject, and this substance is extracted in liquid form, which by their art and by a long digestion <note>In alchemy, digestion refers to the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat—often in a sealed vessel—to mature or "ripen" the material.</note>, they can dry and reduce to powder. its radical moisture or essence. 
Once this essence has been extracted, what remains of the compound is what they call the *impure body*. This usually appears in the form of a tasteless and foul-smelling water they name *phlegm* <note>In alchemy, phlegm is the watery, inactive, and "dead" part of a substance that remains after the vital or spiritual essence has been removed.</note>, or in the form of a tasteless earth. Sometimes, a little of one or the other of these two substances remains, depending on the specific nature of the materials being worked upon.

Because this impure body possesses none of the powers of the mixture <note>original: "mixte." This refers to any natural body composed of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note> and is a stinking matter, they rightly call it the *dead body*. It is deprived of its soul—which is its essence—and retains none of the properties or virtues of the essence that has been stripped from it. For the body is formed of "rainwater" <note>original: "eau pluviale." While literally rainwater, in this context it refers to the common, non-essential liquid portion of a body.</note> and a coarse earth. We will speak more particularly of plants, which will serve as an instruction for the others.

And if you wish to have a greater quantity of essence <note>In this context, the "essence" or "quintessence" is the concentrated medicinal or spiritual core of the plant, believed to be released only after the physical structure is broken down.</note>, and even a better quality, take more fresh sage, crush it like the first batch, place it likewise in a vessel, pour the first already-fermented juice back over it, and let everything ferment together as you did before.

## *On the extraction of mineral essences.*

The bodies of minerals are more compact and drier, and consequently, it is more difficult to extract their essence from them.

One can distinguish between the mineral bodies that are useful to <term>Chemistry</term> <note>Refers to *Chymie*, which during this era (the 17th or 18th century) did not strictly distinguish between modern chemistry and alchemy.</note> and good for health as saline bodies and metallic bodies. One could even distinguish them into several others, and create several classes; but since we can only provide simple insights and the general principles of the <term>Art</term> <note>The "Art" or the "Hermetic Art" is a traditional term for the practice of alchemy.</note>, it is sufficient to treat these two species. If one knows how to handle these well, they will soon know the rest, and then nothing else would be needed.

As for salts, they must be corrupted <note>In an alchemical context, "corruption" or "putrefaction" refers to the process of breaking down a substance's physical structure to release its inner spirit or essence.</note>; and in order that their essen-

It was therefore a true **fixed moon** <note>original: *lune fixe* — In alchemical symbolism, the "Moon" (Luna) represents silver. I wish to state this to disillusion those who allow themselves to be led astray by the false recipes that those miserable **puffers** <term>original: *ſouffleurs* — a derogatory name for amateur or fraudulent alchemists who merely "puffed" at bellows without understanding the true theory</term> bring them every day to involve them in great expenses, having no goal but to secure their own sustenance during operations which they make last as long as [possible...] <note>The text ends mid-sentence at "le plus" (the most/the longest).</note>

## III <gloss>1</gloss> T R A I T I S E .

->*WHETHER a **Universal Medicine** can be found*<-

# Discoveries.

...but even to cure them all; it is necessary beforehand to consider the origin of all ailments in general, and then the nature of the remedy.

Few people are unaware that the origin of **diseases** in general comes from poor **digestions**, which produce **malignant ferments** <note>original: "levains malins"; in early modern medicine, "ferments" or leavens were thought to be active agents in the body that caused chemical changes. Now, they all agree on this point, and they repeat it continually: that in the order of nature, every thing is engendered and multiplied by the **seed** of its own species, and not otherwise. They also tell us at every moment that man begets man, the horse begets the horse, and that only metal can produce metal—and consequently, only gold can produce gold. They are all in agreement on this; we must, therefore, believe them.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the works of Roger Bacon and introduces his contemporary, Richard of England. # Secrets of the Philosophy of the Ancients,

His companion Richard of England, <note>Richardus Anglicus, a 13th-century alchemist often credited with the work *Correctorium Alchemiae* (The Corrector of Alchemy).</note> in his *Correctorium*, clarifies the slight obscurity found in the discourse of the other [Bacon]; or, as it very much appears, Bacon's words were altered by the envious individuals who had them printed. <note>Alchemists often believed that "envious" people intentionally corrupted manuscripts to lead the uninitiated astray.</note> Let us then listen to Richard, who, after having expanded greatly upon the way in which metals are produced in the mines, and having made it known that they are formed from sulfur and quicksilver, <term>original: *soufre & argent-vif*; the two primary principles which alchemists believed combined in the earth to create all metals.</term> continues thus.

Those people are quite foolish, he says, who put forward so many sophistries to deceive men, and so many improbable things that have no foundation in nature. They rave wildly by taking an afterbirth, <note>The placenta; charlatans often used organic waste in "false" alchemy.</note> eggshells, hair, the blood of a redheaded man, <note>Red hair was often associated with sulfurous properties or specific planetary influences in medieval lore.</note> basilisks, <note>A mythical reptile said to be hatched by a toad from a cock's egg; its ashes were rumored to turn copper into gold.</note> worms, and all [kinds...]

<meta>catchword: kinds</meta>

<summary>The text introduces Richard of England's "Correctorium" as a guide to understanding Roger Bacon's work, while denouncing fraudulent alchemists who use absurd, non-natural ingredients.</summary>
<keywords>Richard of England, Roger Bacon, Correctorium Alchemiae, sulfur-mercury theory, alchemical sophistry, metallic generation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the quote from Richard of England’s "Correctorium," listing the absurd materials used by false alchemists before transitioning to his central argument: the principle of "like produces like" in the creation of metals.</meta>

<header>discovered.</header>

kinds of herbs, human excrement, and that of other animals; and thus they claim, using very vile, <note>original: *méchantes*; here meaning base, worthless, or of poor quality.</note> imperfect things so far removed from the subject, to grant perfection to metals. But because in their imaginations they never knew how to recognize what they ought to do, they claimed that by sowing dung <note>original: *fiente*; animal manure or excrement.</note> they could harvest gold; which appears impossible, following the axiom that *your harvest will be similar to what you have sown:* <note>A fundamental alchemical principle mirroring the biblical "reap what you sow," asserting that the "seed" of gold must be gold itself.</note> therefore, he who sows dung will harvest nothing but dung. Thus, it is no wonder if those who wish to perform our wonders with such ugly things, so distant from the metallic nature, are deceived, as are those who put their faith in what they say. Sow, therefore, gold or silver, if you wish them to bring [forth...]

## FIFTH TREATISE

### Concerning Philosophical Mercury and Gold.

THE <term>Chemical Philosophers</term><note>The term used for alchemists who practiced the "Spagyric Art" or laboratory alchemy to uncover the secrets of nature.</note> create nothing new, but they prepare and purify what nature gives them, accompanied by those <term>impurities</term><note>original: "immondices"; in an alchemical context, this refers to the "dross" or coarse matter that hides the true essence of a metal.</note> that are called the *original sin of individuals*, that is to say, an impure stain that accompanies all beings, and from which even mercury and gold are not exempt.

When the Philosophers have reduced these two substances to the purity required for their <term>Great Work</term><note>The *Magnum Opus*, the multi-stage process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, they call it in this state gold and

<summary>The author explains the necessity of a "middle way" in alchemical writing—revealing enough truth for the wise to understand while keeping secrets hidden from the unworthy. He notes that the "great Philosopher" (Geber) provided enough clarity in his writings for diligent, educated students to discover the First Matter and succeed in the Great Work.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, First Matter, Great Work, Geber, secrecy, occult philosophy, preparations, philosophical writings</keywords>

### Secrets of the Philosophy of the Ancients

extract a subtle and penetrating essence, in such a way that it can resolve gold into **quicksilver**<term>quicksilver: also known as mercury; in alchemy, "quicksilver" or "argent-vif" refers to the fluidic principle of metals</term> by said manipulation and purification. From this results the **first quicksilver of the Philosophers**, which is the key that opens the door of the **royal house**<note>The "royal house" is an allegorical term for the body of the gold or the vessel containing the "King" (Gold)</note> where the second and true quicksilver of the Philosophers is locked away. This second quicksilver is not found upon the earth; rather, the Philosopher must bring it into being, composing it from **crude quicksilver** and **cooked quicksilver**—the latter being the "son," more cooked and riper than its "mother." For when gold is resolved into quicksilver by means of the first quicksilver, the union of the two results in a **third quicksilver**, which is, as I have just said, the true **philosophical mercury**.
